### Question 101: Catenins (Beta-Catenin) Linking E-Cadherin to Actin Cytoskeleton
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of β-catenin in cell adhesion, specifically its function as a linker between E-cadherin and the actin cytoskeleton, and the consequences of its dysfunction in development, leading to specific congenital anomalies. (理解β-catenin在细胞粘附中的作用，特别是它作为E-cadherin和肌动蛋白细胞骨架之间的连接体功能，以及其功能障碍在发育中的后果，导致特定的先天性异常。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy is brought to the pediatrician by his parents due to concerns about his physical development. They report that he has always been smaller than his peers and has noticeable physical features. On examination, the child exhibits hypertelorism (widely spaced eyes), a prominent forehead, bilateral sensorineural hearing loss, and mild intellectual disability. His hands show clinodactyly (inward curvature of the fifth finger) and syndactyly (fusion of the second and third toes). A skeletal survey reveals vertebral abnormalities, including hemivertebrae and scoliosis. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *CTNNB1* gene, which encodes β-catenin.

(A) The mutation in *CTNNB1* primarily affects the formation of desmosomes, leading to impaired cell-cell adhesion in the epidermis and neural crest derivatives.
(B) The mutation disrupts the interaction between E-cadherin and the actin cytoskeleton, impairing cell adhesion and migration during embryogenesis, particularly affecting neural crest cells and mesoderm.
(C) The mutation leads to increased expression of N-cadherin, promoting cell-cell adhesion in the developing central nervous system at the expense of E-cadherin-mediated adhesion in epithelial tissues.
(D) The mutation causes a deficiency in plakoglobin, a component of desmosomes and adherens junctions, resulting in weakened cell-cell adhesion throughout the body.
(E) The mutation affects the phosphorylation state of β-catenin, leading to its constitutive degradation via the proteasome pathway, preventing its translocation to the nucleus and subsequent gene transcription.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with developmental delay, hypertelorism, hearing loss, intellectual disability, clinodactyly, syndactyly, vertebral abnormalities. This constellation of findings suggests a genetic syndrome affecting multiple systems, particularly those derived from neural crest and mesoderm.
- **Genetic Finding:** Heterozygous mutation in *CTNNB1* gene, encoding β-catenin. This points directly to a defect in the Wnt signaling pathway and cell adhesion.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the primary mechanism by which the *CTNNB1* mutation causes the observed phenotype.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **β-catenin Function:** β-catenin is a crucial protein involved in both Wnt signaling and cell-cell adhesion.
    - **Adhesion:** In adherens junctions, β-catenin links the transmembrane protein E-cadherin (a calcium-dependent adhesion molecule) to the actin cytoskeleton via α-catenin and other proteins. This complex provides mechanical strength to epithelial and endothelial cell sheets and is essential for cell migration and tissue morphogenesis.
    - **Wnt Signaling:** In the absence of Wnt signaling, β-catenin is continuously phosphorylated by kinases (GSK3β, CK1α, etc.) in the cytoplasm, targeting it for ubiquitination and degradation by the proteasome. When Wnt ligands bind to Frizzled receptors and LRP co-receptors, the destruction complex is inhibited, allowing β-catenin to accumulate in the cytoplasm and translocate to the nucleus. In the nucleus, β-catenin interacts with TCF/LEF transcription factors to activate target genes involved in cell proliferation, differentiation, and survival.
- **CTNNB1 Mutations:** Mutations in *CTNNB1* can affect either its role in adhesion or its role in Wnt signaling. The clinical presentation described (hypertelorism, hearing loss, syndactyly, vertebral defects) is characteristic of syndromes like Crossroads syndrome (also known as Malan syndrome or *CTNNB1*-related disorder), which are associated with mutations affecting the interaction between β-catenin and E-cadherin or the actin cytoskeleton. These mutations often lead to reduced cell adhesion and impaired cell migration during development, particularly affecting neural crest-derived structures (e.g., craniofacial bones, inner ear) and mesoderm (e.g., vertebrae).
- **Option B Analysis:** This option correctly describes the primary function of β-catenin in adherens junctions – linking E-cadherin to the actin cytoskeleton. Disruption of this interaction impairs cell adhesion and migration, explaining the developmental defects observed in the patient, especially those involving neural crest and mesoderm derivatives.
- **Option A Analysis:** Desmosomes are primarily involved in strong cell-cell adhesion, using cadherins like desmoglein and desmocollin, linked to intermediate filaments (keratin). While β-catenin is involved in adherens junctions, not desmosomes, and mutations in desmosomal proteins cause different syndromes (e.g., epidermolysis bullosa simplex, arrhythmogenic right ventricular cardiomyopathy).
- **Option C Analysis:** N-cadherin is involved in cell-cell adhesion, but it typically mediates homophilic binding (N-cadherin to N-cadherin) and is often expressed in mesenchymal cells and neural tissues. Increased N-cadherin expression is not the primary mechanism associated with *CTNNB1* mutations causing this phenotype; rather, the defect lies in the E-cadherin/β-catenin/actin complex.
- **Option D Analysis:** Plakoglobin (also known as γ-catenin) is a component of both adherens junctions and desmosomes. While it interacts with β-catenin, mutations in plakoglobin cause different syndromes (e.g., dilated cardiomyopathy, palmoplantar keratoderma). The primary defect in *CTNNB1* mutations is in β-catenin itself, not plakoglobin.
- **Option E Analysis:** While β-catenin is regulated by phosphorylation and degradation, mutations in *CTNNB1* often affect its ability to bind to E-cadherin or interact with the actin cytoskeleton, rather than solely altering its phosphorylation status and degradation. Although some mutations can affect Wnt signaling, the described phenotype is more consistent with a defect in the adhesion complex. Furthermore, constitutive degradation would likely *reduce* β-catenin levels, whereas the described phenotype results from impaired function of the existing protein in the adhesion complex.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. β-catenin is a key component of adherens junctions, not desmosomes. Desmosomes use different cadherins (desmogleins, desmocollins) and link to intermediate filaments (keratins). Mutations affecting desmosomes cause distinct syndromes.
- (B): Correct. This accurately describes the role of β-catenin in linking E-cadherin to the actin cytoskeleton within adherens junctions. Disruption of this linkage impairs cell adhesion and migration, explaining the developmental defects seen in the patient.
- (C): Incorrect. While cadherins are involved, the primary defect in *CTNNB1* mutations causing this syndrome is not increased N-cadherin expression. The issue is with the E-cadherin/β-catenin/actin complex.
- (D): Incorrect. Plakoglobin (γ-catenin) is a related protein, but the mutation is in *CTNNB1* (β-catenin), not the gene for plakoglobin. Mutations in plakoglobin cause different clinical syndromes.
- (E): Incorrect. While β-catenin is regulated by phosphorylation and degradation, the described clinical phenotype is primarily associated with mutations affecting its interaction with E-cadherin or the actin cytoskeleton, leading to impaired cell adhesion, rather than solely affecting its degradation pathway.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Adherens Junctions (E-cadherin/β-catenin/Actin) | Desmosomes (Desmoglein/Desmocollin/Intermediate Filaments) | Hemidesmosomes (Integrin/Intermediate Filaments) | Gap Junctions (Connexins) |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|---------------------------|
| **Primary Cadherin** | E-cadherin                                      | Desmoglein, Desmocollin                                   | Integrins (e.g., α6β4)                            | Connexins                 |
| **Cytoskeletal Link**| Actin (via α-catenin, β-catenin)                | Intermediate Filaments (Keratins) (via Plakoglobin, Plakophilins) | Intermediate Filaments (Keratins)                 | None (direct channel)     |
| **Function**         | Cell-cell adhesion, tissue morphogenesis, cell migration | Strong cell-cell adhesion, mechanical strength          | Cell-basement membrane adhesion                  | Intercellular communication |
| **Key Protein**      | β-catenin                                       | Plakoglobin, Plakophilins                                 | α6β4 Integrin, Plectin                             | Connexin 43               |
| **Associated Defects**| *CTNNB1* mutations (Crossroads syndrome), APC mutations (FAP) | Desmoglein mutations (Pemphigus vulgaris), Plakophilin mutations (ARVC) | Integrin mutations (Junctional epidermolysis bullosa) | Connexin mutations (various) |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
β-catenin is a critical linker protein in adherens junctions, connecting E-cadherin to the actin cytoskeleton. Mutations in *CTNNB1* can disrupt this linkage, impairing cell adhesion and migration during development, leading to syndromes like Crossroads syndrome characterized by craniofacial, auditory, vertebral, and limb abnormalities. Differentiate this mechanism from defects in desmosomes (e.g., desmoglein mutations causing pemphigus) or intermediate filaments (e.g., keratin mutations causing epidermolysis bullosa simplex).

### Question 102: Loss of E-Cadherin in Diffuse Gastric Cancer & Lobular Breast Cancer
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Loss of E-cadherin function leads to decreased cell-cell adhesion, resulting in characteristic histological features and patterns of spread in certain cancers, including diffuse gastric cancer (signet ring cells) and invasive lobular breast cancer. (E-钙粘蛋白功能丧失导致细胞间粘附力降低，从而导致某些癌症（包括弥漫性胃癌（Signet环细胞）和浸润性小叶乳腺癌）的特征性组织学特征和扩散模式。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old woman with a family history of breast cancer presents for a routine check-up. She reports recent onset of vague abdominal discomfort and early satiety. Physical examination reveals a palpable, firm, non-tender mass in the left upper quadrant. An upper endoscopy shows diffuse thickening of the gastric wall with multiple ulcerated lesions. Biopsy specimens from both the stomach and a previously diagnosed breast lesion (found incidentally on mammogram 2 years ago, initially classified as lobular carcinoma in situ) are examined. Histologically, the gastric biopsy shows poorly differentiated cells with large amounts of intracellular mucin, pushing the nucleus to the periphery, forming characteristic "signet ring" cells. The breast biopsy shows infiltrating nests of small, discohesive cells with minimal glandular formation, often arranged in single-file lines. Immunohistochemical staining for E-cadherin is markedly reduced or absent in both the gastric and breast tumor cells. Which of the following embryological structures is most closely associated with the development of the cell type exhibiting loss of E-cadherin function in both these malignancies?

(A) Neural crest cells
(B) Endoderm-derived epithelium of the primitive gut tube
(C) Mesoderm-derived connective tissue of the gastric wall
(D) Ectoderm-derived epithelium of the mammary gland ducts
(E) Pluripotent cells of the inner cell mass

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old woman with family history of breast cancer. This suggests a potential genetic predisposition, common in both breast and gastric cancers.
- **Symptoms:** Vague abdominal discomfort, early satiety. These are non-specific but point towards a gastric pathology.
- **Physical Exam:** Palpable mass in the left upper quadrant (LUQ). Consistent with gastric involvement.
- **Endoscopy:** Diffuse thickening of the gastric wall, ulcerated lesions. Suggests infiltrative gastric cancer.
- **Histology (Gastric):** "Signet ring" cells (poorly differentiated cells with intracellular mucin pushing the nucleus peripherally). This is characteristic of diffuse-type gastric adenocarcinoma.
- **Histology (Breast):** Infiltrating nests of small, discohesive cells, single-file pattern, minimal glandular formation. This is characteristic of invasive lobular carcinoma (ILC).
- **Immunohistochemistry:** Markedly reduced/absent E-cadherin in both tumor types. This is a key molecular feature linking diffuse gastric cancer and ILC.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the embryological structure most closely associated with the *cell type* exhibiting loss of E-cadherin function in *both* malignancies. The cell type is epithelial (lining the gut and mammary glands).

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **E-cadherin:** A transmembrane glycoprotein crucial for calcium-dependent cell-cell adhesion in epithelial tissues. Its loss leads to decreased cohesion, allowing cells to detach and metastasize more easily.
- **Diffuse Gastric Cancer (Signet Ring):** A subtype of gastric adenocarcinoma characterized by poorly differentiated cells with loss of glandular architecture and often loss of E-cadherin expression. These cells are highly invasive and tend to spread diffusely through the gastric wall (linitis plastica). Embryologically, the gastric epithelium originates from the endoderm lining the primitive gut tube.
- **Invasive Lobular Carcinoma (ILC):** The second most common type of invasive breast cancer. Characterized by the loss of E-cadherin expression, leading to discohesive cells that infiltrate the breast stroma in single-file lines or small nests, often without forming distinct glandular structures. Embryologically, the mammary gland epithelium originates from the ectoderm (specifically, the surface ectoderm) during the formation of the milk line, which then invaginates to form the mammary buds. However, the *epithelial cell type* itself, responsible for the loss of E-cadherin, is fundamentally epithelial, derived from the germ layer that gives rise to epithelial linings.
- **Embryological Origin:** Both the gastric epithelium (lining the stomach) and the mammary gland epithelium (lining the ducts and lobules) are epithelial tissues. Epithelial tissues, regardless of their specific location (gut, breast, skin, etc.), are derived primarily from the ectoderm, mesoderm, or endoderm during embryogenesis. The question asks for the origin of the *cell type* (epithelial cells) showing loss of E-cadherin. The gastric epithelium originates from the endoderm of the primitive gut tube. The mammary epithelium originates from the ectoderm. However, the *cell type* itself (epithelial cells) is fundamentally epithelial, and the gastric epithelium is the most direct example provided in the vignette (signet ring cells). The loss of E-cadherin is a characteristic feature of the *epithelial cells* in both cancers. The gastric epithelium originates directly from the endoderm lining the primitive gut tube. The mammary epithelium originates from the ectoderm. The question asks for the origin of the *cell type* exhibiting the loss of E-cadherin. The gastric epithelial cells (signet ring cells) are explicitly described and are derived from the endoderm. While the breast epithelial cells are also involved, the gastric origin is clearly stated and directly linked to the signet ring morphology. Therefore, the endoderm-derived epithelium of the primitive gut tube is the most appropriate answer, representing the origin of the gastric epithelial cells exhibiting this characteristic loss of E-cadherin.

##### 3. Linking E-cadherin Loss to Embryology
- E-cadherin is crucial for epithelial morphogenesis during development. Its loss can disrupt tissue organization and potentially contribute to cancer development. The epithelial cells lining the gut tube (endoderm) and mammary glands (ectoderm) normally express E-cadherin to maintain tissue integrity. Loss of this function in cancer cells allows them to detach and invade. The question links the *cell type* (epithelial) showing this loss to its embryological origin.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Neural crest cells: These are ectodermal derivatives that migrate to form various structures, including parts of the peripheral nervous system, melanocytes, and adrenal medulla. They are not the origin of the epithelial cells lining the gut or mammary glands. Incorrect.
- (C) Mesoderm-derived connective tissue of the gastric wall: The gastric wall contains mesoderm-derived connective tissue (smooth muscle, fibroblasts), but the epithelial lining (where the signet ring cells arise) is endodermal. Connective tissue does not typically exhibit loss of E-cadherin in this context. Incorrect.
- (D) Ectoderm-derived epithelium of the mammary gland ducts: While the mammary gland epithelium is ectodermal in origin, the vignette focuses heavily on the gastric component (signet ring cells) and the shared molecular feature (loss of E-cadherin). The gastric epithelium is explicitly endodermal. Although ILC is ectodermal, the gastric cancer component points strongly towards the endodermal origin of the *epithelial cell type* in question, especially given the specific description of signet ring cells. Option (B) is a more direct fit for the described gastric pathology. Incorrect.
- (E) Pluripotent cells of the inner cell mass: These cells give rise to all three germ layers (ectoderm, mesoderm, endoderm) and eventually form the entire embryo. While the ultimate origin of all cells, it's too broad and doesn't specify the germ layer origin of the *epithelial cells* involved in these cancers. Incorrect.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Gastric Epithelium (Signet Ring) | Breast Epithelium (ILC) | Neural Crest Cells | Mesoderm (Gastric Wall) | Ectoderm (Mammary) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :---------------------- | :----------------- | :---------------------- | :----------------- |
| **Germ Layer Origin**   | Endoderm                       | Ectoderm                | Ectoderm           | Mesoderm                | Ectoderm           |
| **Cell Type**           | Epithelial                     | Epithelial              | Neural/Pigment/etc. | Connective Tissue       | Epithelial         |
| **E-cadherin Expression** | Lost/Reduced                   | Lost/Reduced            | Variable           | Normal                  | Normal             |
| **Key Feature**         | Signet Ring Cells              | Discohesive, Single File | Migration          | Muscle/Fibroblasts      | Ducts/Lobules      |
| **Relevance to Q stem** | Direct origin of affected cells | Origin of affected cells | Incorrect origin   | Incorrect cell type     | Origin of affected cells |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Loss of E-cadherin is a hallmark of both diffuse gastric cancer (signet ring cells) and invasive lobular breast cancer, leading to decreased cell adhesion and invasive growth patterns. Both epithelial cell types originate from specific germ layers during embryogenesis (endoderm for gastric, ectoderm for mammary). Understanding the embryological origin of tissues helps contextualize developmental abnormalities and cancer biology. Key differentials include other gastric cancer types (intestinal type, which typically retains E-cadherin) and other breast cancer types (ductal carcinoma, which also typically retains E-cadherin).

<a id='question-103'></a>

### Question 103: Colorectal Carcinoma & Wnt Signaling Pathway
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of the APC gene in the Wnt/β-catenin signaling pathway and its association with familial adenomatous polyposis (FAP) and colorectal carcinoma, specifically focusing on the anatomical location and clinical presentation related to the adenomatous polyps. (理解 APC 基因在 Wnt/β-catenin 信号通路中的作用及其与结直肠癌和家族性腺瘤病 (FAP) 的关联，特别是关注腺瘤息肉的解剖位置和临床表现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of intermittent rectal bleeding and abdominal discomfort for the past 6 months. He reports passing several small, dark-colored stools. His father was diagnosed with colon cancer at age 50. A colonoscopy reveals hundreds of adenomatous polyps distributed throughout the colon, predominantly in the left colon and rectum, but also present in the small intestine. Biopsy of a polyp confirms adenocarcinoma in situ. Genetic testing reveals a mutation in the APC gene. Which of the following anatomical locations is MOST likely to be affected FIRST by the development of adenomatous polyps in this patient?

(A) Cecum
(B) Ascending Colon
(C) Transverse Colon
(D) Sigmoid Colon
(E) Appendix

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male with rectal bleeding, abdominal discomfort, and family history of colon cancer (father diagnosed at 50). This suggests a potential hereditary colorectal cancer syndrome.
- **Colonoscopy Findings:** Hundreds of adenomatous polyps throughout the colon and small intestine. This is characteristic of Familial Adenomatous Polyposis (FAP).
- **Biopsy:** Adenocarcinoma in situ within a polyp. This confirms the malignant potential of the polyps.
- **Genetic Testing:** Mutation in the APC gene. This confirms the diagnosis of FAP. The APC gene is a tumor suppressor gene involved in the Wnt/β-catenin signaling pathway. Loss-of-function mutations lead to constitutive activation of β-catenin, promoting cell proliferation and polyp formation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the anatomical location MOST likely affected FIRST by adenomatous polyps in FAP.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **FAP and Polyp Distribution:** FAP is characterized by the development of hundreds to thousands of adenomatous polyps throughout the gastrointestinal tract, particularly the colon and rectum.
- **Typical Distribution Pattern:** While polyps can occur anywhere from the stomach to the rectum, the highest density is typically found in the distal colon, specifically the rectum and sigmoid colon. The proximal colon (cecum, ascending colon, transverse colon) tends to have fewer polyps, although they are still commonly affected. The small intestine (duodenum, jejunum, ileum) can also be involved, particularly in the periampullary region and duodenum.
- **Anatomical Considerations:** The rectum and sigmoid colon are the terminal parts of the large intestine, representing the final segment of the digestive tract before the anus. Their location and potentially different microenvironment might influence the initial site of polyp formation in FAP.
- **Clinical Relevance:** The high polyp burden in the distal colon and rectum significantly increases the risk of colorectal cancer, often necessitating prophylactic colectomy. The typical progression involves the rectum and sigmoid colon being affected earliest and most severely.
- **Wnt/β-catenin Pathway:** The APC protein normally degrades β-catenin. When APC is mutated, β-catenin accumulates in the cytoplasm and translocates to the nucleus, where it activates genes promoting cell proliferation, leading to adenoma formation. This pathway is crucial for normal intestinal stem cell maintenance and differentiation, and its dysregulation is a hallmark of FAP.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Cecum: The cecum is the beginning of the large intestine. While polyps can occur here in FAP, it is typically not the site of the earliest or most numerous polyps compared to the distal colon.
- (B) Ascending Colon: Similar to the cecum, the ascending colon is part of the proximal colon and usually has fewer polyps than the distal colon in FAP.
- (C) Transverse Colon: The transverse colon is also part of the proximal/mid colon and generally has a lower polyp density compared to the sigmoid colon and rectum in FAP.
- (D) Sigmoid Colon: The sigmoid colon, along with the rectum, is part of the distal colon. This region typically exhibits the highest density of adenomatous polyps in patients with FAP, often being the site of the earliest and most significant polyp burden.
- (E) Appendix: The appendix is a small outpouching from the cecum. While polyps can occur in the appendix, it is not the primary or earliest site affected in FAP compared to the main colon segments, especially the distal ones.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Location        | Typical Polyp Density in FAP | Relative Timing of Onset | Embryological Origin (Hindgut) |
|-----------------|-----------------------------|--------------------------|--------------------------------|
| Cecum           | Low to Moderate             | Later                    | Hindgut                         |
| Ascending Colon | Low to Moderate             | Later                    | Hindgut                         |
| Transverse Colon| Moderate                    | Intermediate             | Hindgut                         |
| Sigmoid Colon   | High                        | Earlier                  | Hindgut                         |
| Rectum          | High                        | Earlier                  | Hindgut                         |
| Appendix        | Low                         | Later                    | Hindgut                         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Familial Adenomatous Polyposis (FAP), caused by APC mutations, leads to numerous adenomatous polyps primarily in the distal colon (rectum and sigmoid). Understanding the typical distribution pattern of polyps in FAP is crucial for diagnosis and management. Differential diagnoses for multiple colorectal polyps include other polyposis syndromes (e.g., MUTYH-associated polyposis, Peutz-Jeghers syndrome) and inflammatory bowel disease, but the specific genetic mutation (APC) and polyp characteristics point strongly towards FAP with a distal predominance.

<a id='question-104'></a>

### Question 104: Skin Biopsy & Ultrastructural Defect
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the ultrastructural components of cell junctions, specifically the macula adherens (desmosome), and its clinical relevance in blistering skin diseases. The key is identifying the specific protein complex responsible for the "spot weld" appearance and its association with a particular genetic disorder. (考察细胞连接的超微结构，特别是粘着斑（desmosome），以及它在皮肤大疱性疾病中的临床相关性。重点是识别负责“点焊”外观的特定蛋白质复合物及其与特定遗传性疾病的关联。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent, painful blisters on his hands, feet, knees, and elbows, appearing after minor trauma or friction. His skin biopsy reveals acantholysis (separation of keratinocytes) within the epidermis, specifically suprabasally. Electron microscopy of the blister fluid shows numerous keratinocytes with detached cell membranes. Ultrastructural examination of the remaining epidermal cells reveals a deficiency in dense plaque structures connecting adjacent keratinocytes, characterized by the absence of cadherin filaments anchoring to intermediate filaments within the cytoplasm. Genetic testing confirms a mutation in the *DSP* gene.

Which of the following ultrastructural components is most likely deficient in this patient's epidermal cells, leading to the observed clinical and microscopic findings?

(A) Hemidesmosomes
(B) Gap junctions
(C) Adherens junctions (zonula adherens)
(D) Macula adherens (desmosome)
(E) Tight junctions (zonula occludens)

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with recurrent, painful blisters on friction-prone areas (hands, feet, knees, elbows) after minor trauma. This suggests a genetic blistering disorder affecting keratinocyte adhesion.
- **Microscopy:** Acantholysis (separation of keratinocytes) within the epidermis, specifically suprabasally. This points to a defect in cell-cell adhesion within the epidermis, above the basal layer.
- **Electron Microscopy:** Detached keratinocytes in blister fluid. This confirms the loss of cell adhesion.
- **Ultrastructural Examination:** Deficiency in dense plaque structures connecting adjacent keratinocytes, absence of cadherin filaments anchoring to intermediate filaments. This is the key ultrastructural finding. Dense plaques are characteristic of desmosomes. The anchoring of cadherins (desmogleins/desmocollins) to intermediate filaments (keratins) via the plaque is crucial for desmosome function.
- **Genetic Testing:** Mutation in the *DSP* gene. The *DSP* gene encodes desmoplakin, a major component of the desmosomal plaque. Mutations in *DSP* cause arrhythmogenic right ventricular cardiomyopathy (ARVC) and palmoplantar ectodermal dysplasia (PPED), which presents with skin fragility and blistering. The skin findings described are consistent with PPED.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific ultrastructural component deficient based on the clinical, histological, and ultrastructural findings.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Desmosomes (Macula Adherens):** These are cell junctions that provide strong adhesion between adjacent cells, particularly in tissues subjected to mechanical stress, like the epidermis. They are characterized by dense plaques on the cytoplasmic side of the plasma membrane, composed of proteins like desmoplakin, plakoglobin, and plakophilin. Transmembrane cadherins (desmogleins and desmocollins) link to these plaque proteins, which in turn bind to intermediate filaments (keratins in epithelial cells). This creates a strong "spot weld" connection.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's presentation (blistering after trauma, suprabasal acantholysis) and the ultrastructural finding (deficient dense plaques, lack of cadherin-intermediate filament anchoring) are classic features of defects in desmosomal function. Mutations in genes encoding desmosomal proteins (e.g., *DSP* for desmoplakin, *DSG* genes for desmogleins) cause various blistering disorders, including PPED.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** The clinical picture and suprabasal acantholysis differentiate this from other blistering diseases. Pemphigus vulgaris involves autoantibodies against desmoglein 3, causing suprabasal acantholysis, but the ultrastructural defect is the *loss* of desmosomes due to antibody attack, not a primary genetic defect in the desmosome structure itself. Bullous pemphigoid involves subepidermal blistering due to antibodies against hemidesmosomal proteins (BP180, BP230). Epidermolysis bullosa simplex involves defects in keratin intermediate filaments or associated proteins, leading to intraepidermal cleavage, but the desmosomes themselves are structurally intact.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Hemidesmosomes:** These junctions anchor basal keratinocytes to the basement membrane. Defects in hemidesmosomes (e.g., in bullous pemphigoid or junctional epidermolysis bullosa) cause subepidermal or junctional blistering, respectively, not suprabasal acantholysis. Ultrastructurally, they appear as dense plaques but are located at the basal cell surface and connect to the basement membrane, not to adjacent keratinocytes.
- (B) **Gap junctions:** These are channels that allow direct communication and passage of small molecules between adjacent cells. They are important for coordinated cellular activity but do not provide significant mechanical adhesion. Defects in gap junctions are not typically associated with blistering skin diseases. Ultrastructurally, they appear as arrays of transmembrane channels (connexons).
- (C) **Adherens junctions (zonula adherens):** These junctions form a continuous belt around cells, typically located just below the tight junctions. They provide mechanical adhesion and are involved in cell signaling. They contain cadherins (E-cadherin in epithelial cells) linked to actin filaments via catenins. While they contribute to cell adhesion, they are not the primary structures responsible for the strong "spot weld" adhesion seen in desmosomes, nor do they typically exhibit the dense plaque structure described. Defects are associated with loss of cell polarity and altered tissue architecture, not typically primary blistering.
- (E) **Tight junctions (zonula occludens):** These junctions form a seal between adjacent cells, preventing paracellular leakage. They are located at the apical-most part of the cell membrane. They do not provide significant mechanical adhesion. Defects are associated with increased permeability, not blistering due to loss of cell-cell adhesion. Ultrastructurally, they appear as fused strands spanning the intercellular space.

#### High-Yield Anatomital Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Desmosome (Macula Adherens) | Hemidesmosome | Adherens Junction (Zonula Adherens) | Gap Junction | Tight Junction (Zonula Occludens) |
|----------------------|-----------------------------|----------------|-----------------------------------|--------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Location**         | Cell-Cell (lateral)         | Cell-Basement Membrane | Cell-Cell (belt-like, below tight jxn) | Cell-Cell    | Cell-Cell (apical)                |
| **Primary Function** | Strong mechanical adhesion  | Cell-Matrix Adhesion | Mechanical adhesion, signaling     | Communication | Barrier function (paracellular seal) |
| **Ultrastructure**   | Dense plaque, cadherins (desmogleins/desmocollins), intermediate filaments (keratins) | Dense plaque, integrins (e.g., BP180, BP230), lamina densa | Cadherins (E-cadherin), catenins, actin filaments | Connexons (channels) | Claudins, occludins, JAMs             |
| **Key Proteins**     | Desmoplakin, Desmogleins, Desmocollins | BP180, BP230, Integrins | E-cadherin, Catenins              | Connexin    | Claudin, Occludin                  |
| **Clinical Defect**  | Blistering (e.g., PPED, Pemphigus) | Subepidermal blistering (e.g., Bullous Pemphigoid) | Altered cell polarity, tissue architecture | Rare association with disease | Increased permeability                |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Desmosomes (macula adherens) are crucial for epidermal integrity, providing strong cell-cell adhesion via cadherin-intermediate filament linkages within dense cytoplasmic plaques. Genetic defects in desmosomal proteins (like desmoplakin encoded by *DSP*) lead to skin fragility and blistering diseases like palmoplantar ectodermal dysplasia (PPED). Differentiating desmosomes from other cell junctions (hemidesmosomes, adherens junctions, gap junctions, tight junctions) based on location, function, ultrastructure, and associated clinical syndromes is key.

<a id='question-105'></a>

### Question 105: Desmoglein-1 and Desmoglein-3 Transmembrane Glycoproteins
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the differential expression of desmoglein-1 (DSG1) and desmoglein-3 (DSG3) transmembrane glycoproteins in the epidermis, their role in cell-cell adhesion, and the clinical manifestations resulting from autoantibodies targeting these proteins, specifically in the context of pemphigus vulgaris and pemphigus foliaceus. (考察学生对表皮中整合素1 (DSG1) 和整合素3 (DSG3) 跨膜糖蛋白的差异表达、它们在细胞-细胞粘附中的作用，以及针对这些蛋白的自身抗体所导致的临床表现的理解，特别是关于天疱疮和叶状天疱疮。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old female presents to the dermatology clinic with a 3-month history of painful, rapidly progressing oral lesions and widespread blistering on her skin, particularly affecting the trunk and proximal extremities. The blisters are flaccid and easily rupture, leaving behind painful erosions. Physical examination reveals multiple erythematous macules, papules, and flaccid bullae of varying sizes on the trunk and upper arms. Oral mucosa examination shows extensive erosions and ulcerations. A skin biopsy is performed from a perilesional area. Histopathology reveals intraepidermal acantholysis (separation of keratinocytes within the epidermis) with Tzanck cells (acantholytic keratinocytes) present. Direct immunofluorescence (DIF) microscopy of the perilesional skin shows intercellular deposition of IgG antibodies throughout the epidermis. Indirect immunofluorescence (IIF) testing on patient serum shows antibodies binding to the cell surface of keratinocytes. ELISA testing confirms the presence of IgG autoantibodies specifically targeting desmoglein-3 (DSG3) and desmoglein-1 (DSG1). Given the widespread skin and mucosal involvement, which layer of the epidermis is primarily affected by the loss of cell-cell adhesion in this patient?

(A) Stratum basale
(B) Stratum spinosum
(C) Stratum granulosum
(D) Stratum lucidum
(E) Stratum corneum

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 52-year-old female with painful, rapidly progressing oral lesions and widespread blistering on skin (trunk, proximal extremities). Blisters are flaccid, rupture easily, leaving erosions. This points towards an autoimmune blistering disease affecting keratinocyte adhesion.
- **Physical Examination:** Flaccid bullae, erosions, extensive oral mucosal involvement. Flaccid bullae suggest intraepidermal cleavage, often seen in pemphigus. Mucosal involvement is common in pemphigus vulgaris.
- **Histopathology:** Intraepidermal acantholysis (separation of keratinocytes within the epidermis) with Tzanck cells. This confirms a loss of cell-cell adhesion within the epidermis.
- **Immunofluorescence:** DIF shows intercellular IgG deposition throughout the epidermis. IIF shows antibodies binding to keratinocytes. ELISA confirms antibodies against DSG3 and DSG1. This confirms an autoimmune process targeting desmosomal proteins.
- **Diagnosis:** The combination of widespread skin and mucosal blistering, intraepidermal acantholysis, and antibodies against both DSG3 and DSG1 is characteristic of pemphigus vulgaris.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which layer of the epidermis is *primarily* affected by the loss of cell-cell adhesion. Pemphigus vulgaris involves acantholysis throughout the epidermis, but the cleavage often occurs suprabasally (just above the basal layer) or within the mid-epidermis. The question asks for the *primary* layer affected by the loss of adhesion.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Epidermal Layers:** The epidermis consists of five layers (from deep to superficial): Stratum basale (basal layer), Stratum spinosum (prickle cell layer), Stratum granulosum (granular layer), Stratum lucidum (clear layer, only in thick skin), and Stratum corneum (horny layer).
- **Desmosomes:** These are cell-cell adhesion structures crucial for maintaining epidermal integrity. They are particularly abundant in the stratum spinosum.
- **Desmogleins (DSG1 & DSG3):** These are transmembrane glycoproteins that are key components of desmosomes.
- **DSG3 Distribution:** Primarily expressed in the lower epidermis (stratum basale and lower stratum spinosum). Antibodies against DSG3 cause cleavage within the lower epidermis, often leading to suprabasal acantholysis, characteristic of pemphigus vulgaris. Mucosal involvement is primarily due to DSG3 targeting.
- **DSG1 Distribution:** Expressed more widely throughout the epidermis, particularly in the upper stratum spinosum and stratum granulosum. Antibodies against DSG1 cause cleavage higher in the epidermis, often within the stratum granulosum or stratum spinosum. Skin involvement in pemphigus vulgaris is often due to DSG1 targeting, in addition to DSG3.
- **Pemphigus Vulgaris Pathophysiology:** Autoantibodies target DSG3 (causing mucosal and suprabasal skin lesions) and often DSG1 (causing more extensive skin lesions, typically higher in the epidermis). The loss of adhesion leads to acantholysis. The level of cleavage determines the clinical appearance (flaccid bullae vs. tense bullae). In pemphigus vulgaris, the cleavage is typically intraepidermal, often suprabasal or within the stratum spinosum, due to the targeting of DSG3 and DSG1.
- **Answering the Question:** The question asks which layer is *primarily* affected by the loss of cell-cell adhesion. While cleavage can occur at different levels, the stratum spinosum is where desmosomes are most abundant and where both DSG1 and DSG3 are significantly expressed. Acantholysis, the hallmark of pemphigus, is the separation of keratinocytes, which occurs most prominently within this layer due to the disruption of desmosomal function by autoantibodies against DSG1 and DSG3. The basal layer (A) is less affected initially, as DSG3 is more concentrated lower down, but the primary site of separation and the layer most characteristic of the disease process (stratum spinosum) is where the bulk of the acantholysis occurs. The granular layer (C), lucidum (D), and corneum (E) are superficial layers and less directly involved in the primary desmosomal disruption causing the characteristic acantholysis of pemphigus vulgaris, although cleavage can extend into these layers depending on the specific antibodies and disease severity.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Stratum basale: While DSG3 is expressed here, the primary cleavage in pemphigus vulgaris often occurs *above* the basal layer (suprabasal acantholysis). The basal cells remain attached to the basement membrane, forming the "tombstone" appearance. Therefore, this layer is not the *primary* site of separation.
- (C) Stratum granulosum: Cleavage can occur here, especially if DSG1 antibodies are present, but the stratum spinosum is a more extensive layer and the site of abundant desmosomes and significant acantholysis involving both DSG1 and DSG3.
- (D) Stratum lucidum: This layer is only present in thick skin (palms, soles) and is not the primary site of acantholysis in generalized pemphigus vulgaris.
- (E) Stratum corneum: This is the outermost layer of dead, keratinized cells. While the epidermis is disrupted, the primary loss of cell-cell adhesion occurs within the living layers below, specifically due to the breakdown of desmosomes connecting keratinocytes.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Layer          | Key Features                                  | DSG Expression | Primary Site of Acantholysis in Pemphigus Vulgaris? |
|----------------|-----------------------------------------------|----------------|---------------------------------------------------|
| Stratum Basale | Single layer, mitotically active keratinocytes | Primarily DSG3 | No (Suprabasal cleavage common)                   |
| Stratum Spinosum| Multiple layers, desmosomes abundant, "spiny" appearance | DSG1 & DSG3   | Yes (Major site of acantholysis)                  |
| Stratum Granulosum| Keratohyalin granules, lamellar bodies        | Primarily DSG1 | Yes (Can be site of cleavage, especially with DSG1 Ab) |
| Stratum Lucidum | Clear layer, only in thick skin                | Low            | No                                                |
| Stratum Corneum | Dead, keratinized cells                       | None           | No                                                |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Pemphigus vulgaris involves autoantibodies against DSG3 and DSG1, leading to intraepidermal acantholysis, primarily within the stratum spinosum, resulting in flaccid bullae and erosions affecting both skin and mucous membranes. Understanding the differential expression of desmogleins helps localize the level of epidermal cleavage in autoimmune blistering diseases. Key differentials include pemphigus foliaceus (primarily DSG1, superficial cleavage), bullous pemphigoid (subepidermal cleavage), and dermatitis herpetiformis (subepidermal neutrophilic microabscesses).

<a id='question-106'></a>

### Question 106: Cardiac Myocyte Adhesion & Arrhythmogenesis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the structure and function of desmosomes, specifically the plakoglobin-containing adhesion plaques (desmoplakin, plakoglobin, plakophilin) within cardiac myocytes, and the clinical consequences of mutations affecting these proteins, leading to arrhythmogenic cardiomyopathy (ARVC). (考察心肌细胞中粘附斑 (desmosomes) 的结构和功能，特别是含有 plakoglobin 的粘附斑 (desmoplakin, plakoglobin, plakophilin)，以及影响这些蛋白质突变的临床后果，导致致心律失常性心肌病 (ARVC))

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male marathon runner collapses during a race. He is immediately resuscitated with CPR and defibrillation for ventricular fibrillation. His past medical history is unremarkable, and his family history is significant for sudden cardiac death in his younger brother at age 28 during soccer practice. An electrocardiogram (ECG) shows frequent premature ventricular contractions (PVCs) and T-wave inversions in the right precordial leads (V1-V3). An echocardiogram reveals mild right ventricular dilation and hypokinesis, particularly in the apical and outflow regions. Cardiac MRI shows fatty infiltration of the right ventricular myocardium, replacing the myocytes. Endomyocardial biopsy reveals fragmented myocytes with loss of intercellular connections and fibrofatty replacement. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *JUP* gene, which encodes plakoglobin.

Which of the following cellular structures is most directly affected by the mutation in the *JUP* gene, leading to the patient's condition?

(A) Z-discs
(B) Intermediate filaments (Desmin)
(C) Adherens junctions (E-cadherin)
(D) Gap junctions (Connexin 43)
(E) Desmosomes (Plakoglobin-containing adhesion plaque)

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** Young male athlete collapsing during exertion, suggesting a structural or electrical heart abnormality exacerbated by stress.
- **Resuscitation:** Ventricular fibrillation (VF) indicates a life-threatening arrhythmia.
- **Family History:** Sudden cardiac death in a young sibling points towards a genetic predisposition, possibly a channelopathy or cardiomyopathy.
- **ECG Findings:** PVCs and T-wave inversions in V1-V3 are characteristic signs associated with right ventricular involvement, often seen in ARVC.
- **Echocardiogram:** Right ventricular dilation and hypokinesis further support ARVC.
- **Cardiac MRI:** Fatty infiltration of the right ventricle is a hallmark feature of ARVC, replacing functional myocardium with adipose and fibrous tissue.
- **Endomyocardial Biopsy:** Fragmented myocytes and loss of intercellular connections are histological findings consistent with ARVC, indicating structural weakness of the cardiac tissue. Fibrofatty replacement confirms the MRI findings.
- **Genetic Testing:** Mutation in the *JUP* gene, encoding plakoglobin, directly links the pathology to a specific protein involved in cell adhesion.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific cellular structure affected by the plakoglobin mutation.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **ARVC Pathophysiology:** Arrhythmogenic Right Ventricular Cardiomyopathy (ARVC) is a genetic heart muscle disease characterized by fibrofatty replacement of the right ventricular myocardium, leading to ventricular arrhythmias and sudden cardiac death.
- **Desmosomes:** Desmosomes are cell-cell adhesion structures crucial for maintaining tissue integrity, especially in tissues subjected to mechanical stress, like the heart muscle. They link the intermediate filament cytoskeletons of adjacent cells.
- **Plakoglobin (JUP):** Plakoglobin is a key component of the desmosomal adhesion plaque. It links desmoplakin (which binds to intermediate filaments) to plakophilin (which binds to cadherins in the cell membrane). This complex provides mechanical strength to the desmosome.
- **Desmoplakin:** Another crucial protein in the desmosomal plaque, directly linking intermediate filaments (desmin in cardiac myocytes) to the plaque complex. Mutations in *DSP* (desmoplakin gene) are the most common cause of ARVC.
- **Plakophilin:** Connects the adhesion plaque to the transmembrane cadherin molecules (desmoglein and desmocollin).
- **Role of Plakoglobin in ARVC:** Mutations in *JUP* (plakoglobin) disrupt the integrity of the desmosomal adhesion plaque, weakening the connection between cardiac myocytes. This leads to cell detachment, myocyte death, and replacement by fibrous and fatty tissue, particularly in the right ventricle, predisposing to arrhythmias like ventricular fibrillation.
- **Other Structures:**
    - **Z-discs:** Structural proteins within the sarcomere, anchoring actin filaments. Not directly involved in cell-cell adhesion in the context of ARVC.
    - **Intermediate filaments (Desmin):** Form the cytoskeleton within myocytes and link to desmosomes via desmoplakin. While desmin mutations can cause cardiomyopathy, the *JUP* mutation specifically affects the adhesion plaque.
    - **Adherens junctions (E-cadherin):** Primarily involved in cell-cell adhesion in epithelial tissues. While some overlap exists, desmosomes are the primary adhesion structures in cardiac muscle. E-cadherin is not the main cadherin in cardiac desmosomes (desmoglein/desmocollin are).
    - **Gap junctions (Connexin 43):** Form channels between adjacent myocytes, allowing electrical coupling and coordinated contraction. Mutations in connexin genes cause different types of cardiomyopathies (e.g., Naxos disease), not typically ARVC.

##### 3. Synthesis:
The clinical presentation (young athlete, sudden collapse, VF), family history, ECG findings (RV abnormalities), imaging (RV dilation, fatty infiltration), biopsy (fragmented myocytes, loss of connections), and specific genetic mutation (*JUP* gene encoding plakoglobin) all converge on a diagnosis of ARVC caused by defective desmosomal adhesion. Plakoglobin is a critical component of the desmosomal adhesion plaque, linking desmoplakin to plakophilin. Therefore, the mutation directly affects the desmosomes.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Z-discs:** These are structural components of the sarcomere, involved in anchoring actin filaments. While crucial for muscle contraction, they are not the primary site of cell-cell adhesion affected in ARVC caused by desmosomal protein mutations. Mutations in Z-disc proteins (e.g., alpha-actinin) cause limb-girdle muscular dystrophy or dilated cardiomyopathy, not ARVC.
- (B) **Intermediate filaments (Desmin):** Desmin forms the intermediate filament network within cardiac myocytes and connects to desmosomes via desmoplakin. Mutations in the *DES* gene (encoding desmin) cause desminopathies, a form of cardiomyopathy, but the specific mutation here is in *JUP* (plakoglobin), which is part of the adhesion plaque itself, not the intermediate filament. While desmosomes link to intermediate filaments, the primary defect in this case is within the plaque complex.
- (C) **Adherens junctions (E-cadherin):** Adherens junctions are primarily found in epithelial tissues and use E-cadherin. Cardiac myocytes rely mainly on desmosomes for strong cell-cell adhesion. While some cadherins (desmoglein, desmocollin) are present in cardiac desmosomes, E-cadherin is not the primary component, and adherens junctions are not the main structure implicated in ARVC pathogenesis related to plakoglobin mutations.
- (D) **Gap junctions (Connexin 43):** Gap junctions are responsible for electrical coupling between cardiac myocytes, allowing rapid spread of action potentials. Mutations in connexin genes (e.g., *GJA1* encoding connexin 43) cause arrhythmogenic cardiomyopathies, but these typically present differently (e.g., Naxos disease) and are not associated with the fibrofatty replacement characteristic of ARVC or mutations in *JUP*.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Key Components                                  | Function                                                                 | Relevance to ARVC (JUP mutation) |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Desmosomes (Correct)** | Desmoglein, Desmocollin, Plakoglobin (JUP), Plakophilin, Desmoplakin | Strong cell-cell adhesion, mechanical integrity, linking intermediate filaments | **Directly affected by JUP mutation, leading to weakened adhesion, myocyte detachment, fibrofatty replacement.** |
| Z-discs               | Alpha-actinin, Myomesin, etc.                   | Anchoring actin filaments within the sarcomere                            | Not directly involved in ARVC pathogenesis related to JUP. |
| Intermediate Filaments | Desmin                                          | Cytoskeletal support, linking desmosomes to sarcomeres                  | Not directly affected by JUP mutation. |
| Adherens Junctions    | E-cadherin, Catenins                             | Cell-cell adhesion (primarily epithelial)                                 | Not the primary structure affected in ARVC. |
| Gap Junctions         | Connexin 43                                     | Electrical coupling between cells                                         | Mutations cause different cardiomyopathies, not typically ARVC. |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
ARVC is often caused by mutations in genes encoding desmosomal proteins (DSP, DSG2, JUP, PKP2, DSC2), leading to weakened cell-cell adhesion in the right ventricle. Plakoglobin (encoded by *JUP*) is a key component of the desmosomal adhesion plaque. Remember to differentiate ARVC (desmosomal defects, fibrofatty replacement) from other cardiomyopathies caused by mutations in sarcomeric proteins (e.g., hypertrophic cardiomyopathy) or gap junction proteins (e.g., Naxos disease).

<a id='question-107'></a>

### Question 107: Epidermolysis Bullosa Simplex / Desmosomal Linkage
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the structural integrity of the epidermis, specifically the connection between keratin intermediate filaments (tonofilaments) and desmosomes via plaque proteins (plakoglobin, plakophilins, desmoplakin), and the clinical consequences of defects in these linkage proteins, leading to Epidermolysis Bullosa Simplex (EBS). (考察表皮结构完整性，特别是角蛋白中间丝（角丝）与半桥连接体之间的连接，以及连接蛋白（斑桥蛋白、斑桥蛋白、角斑蛋白）缺陷的后果，导致单纯性天疱疮 (EBS)。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the dermatology clinic with a history of recurrent blistering on his hands, feet, knees, and elbows since infancy. The blisters are typically non-inflammatory and heal without scarring, although hyperpigmentation is noted in previously affected areas. His mother reports that the blisters occur easily with minor trauma, such as crawling or playing. Physical examination reveals tense, superficial blisters on the palms and soles, and mild erythema surrounding the lesions. A skin biopsy is performed for histological examination. Immunofluorescence studies are negative for antibodies against desmogleins, desmocollins, or hemidesmosomal components. Electron microscopy reveals a separation within the basal epidermis, specifically at the level of the basal keratinocytes, with no cleavage below the basement membrane zone. Genetic testing confirms a heterozygous mutation in the *KRT5* gene.

Which of the following molecular linkages is most likely disrupted in this patient's epidermis, leading to blister formation?

(A) The connection between hemidesmosomes and the basement membrane.
(B) The linkage between keratin intermediate filaments and the nuclear lamina.
(C) The interaction between desmosomal cadherins (desmogleins and desmocollins) and the extracellular matrix.
(D) The attachment of keratin intermediate filaments (tonofilaments) to the cytoplasmic plaque of desmosomes.
(E) The connection between lamellar bodies and the plasma membrane of keratinocytes.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with recurrent, non-inflammatory blisters on hands, feet, knees, elbows since infancy, triggered by minor trauma, healing without scarring but with hyperpigmentation. This clinical picture is highly suggestive of Epidermolysis Bullosa (EB).
- **Physical Examination:** Tense, superficial blisters on palms/soles. This points towards an intraepidermal cleavage plane.
- **Histology/Microscopy:** Skin biopsy shows separation within the basal epidermis at the level of basal keratinocytes. Electron microscopy confirms this intraepidermal split, specifically within the basal layer, without basement membrane involvement. This localizes the defect to the connections within the basal keratinocytes.
- **Immunofluorescence:** Negative for antibodies against desmogleins, desmocollins, hemidesmosomal components. This rules out autoimmune blistering diseases like pemphigus vulgaris (targeting desmogleins) or bullous pemphigoid (targeting hemidesmosomal components).
- **Genetic Testing:** Heterozygous mutation in *KRT5* gene. Keratin 5 (KRT5) is a type I keratin expressed in the basal layer of the epidermis. Mutations in KRT5 (or KRT14) are the most common cause of Epidermolysis Bullosa Simplex (EBS).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the disrupted molecular linkage responsible for blister formation. Given the clinical presentation, histology (intraepidermal split in basal layer), negative immunofluorescence, and KRT5 mutation, the defect lies in the structural integrity of the basal keratinocytes, specifically the connection between the keratin filaments and the desmosomes.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Epidermis Structure:** The epidermis is primarily composed of keratinocytes connected by various cell junctions. Desmosomes are crucial for cell-cell adhesion, particularly in tissues subject to mechanical stress like the epidermis. Hemidesmosomes anchor basal keratinocytes to the basement membrane.
- **Desmosomes:** These are complex structures containing cadherins (desmogleins and desmocollins) that span the intercellular space, linking adjacent cells. Intracellularly, these cadherins are connected to dense cytoplasmic plaques containing plakoglobin, plakophilins, and desmoplakin.
- **Keratin Intermediate Filaments (Tonofilaments):** These filaments provide mechanical strength to keratinocytes. In the basal layer, they are anchored to the nuclear lamina and also to the cytoplasmic plaque of desmosomes via desmoplakin.
- **EBS Pathophysiology:** EBS is characterized by defects in the proteins that link keratin intermediate filaments to desmosomes or to the hemidesmosomes. The most common forms (EBS simplex, formerly known as Weber-Cockayne disease) are caused by mutations in KRT5 or KRT14, which encode keratin filaments found in basal keratinocytes. These mutations disrupt the interaction between the keratin filaments and the desmosomal plaque proteins (specifically desmoplakin), leading to fragile cell-cell adhesion and blister formation upon minor trauma. The cleavage occurs within the basal layer, specifically at the level of the desmosomes or the linkage points.
- **Molecular Linkage:** The critical linkage disrupted in EBS simplex involves the connection between the keratin intermediate filaments (tonofilaments) and the cytoplasmic plaque of the desmosome, primarily mediated by desmoplakin. Desmoplakin binds to both keratin filaments and the desmosomal plaque proteins (plakoglobin, plakophilins).
- **Differential Diagnosis:** Other forms of EBS involve defects in other linkage proteins (e.g., plectin in EBS with muscular dystrophy, BPAG1/BP230 in EBS herpetiformis) or hemidesmosomal components (junctional EB). Dystrophic EB involves defects in type VII collagen in the basement membrane zone.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): The connection between hemidesmosomes and the basement membrane involves integrin α6β4 and components of the basement membrane (like type VII collagen). Defects here cause Junctional Epidermolysis Bullosa (JEB) or Dystrophic Epidermolysis Bullosa (DEB), respectively, characterized by cleavage below the basal keratinocytes at the lamina lucida or within the lamina densa, not within the basal layer as seen in this case.
- (B): The linkage between keratin intermediate filaments and the nuclear lamina is mediated by nesprins and SUN proteins. While important for nuclear positioning and cytoskeletal integrity, defects here are not the primary cause of EBS. Mutations in lamins (like LMNA) cause laminopathies, affecting various tissues including muscle and adipose tissue, not typically causing EBS.
- (C): The interaction between desmosomal cadherins (desmogleins and desmocollins) and the extracellular matrix is incorrect. Cadherins mediate cell-cell adhesion by binding to cadherins on adjacent cells, not the extracellular matrix. Defects in desmogleins (Dsg1, Dsg3) cause Pemphigus Vulgaris and Pemphigus Foliaceus, autoimmune diseases targeting desmosomes, leading to suprabasal or subcorneal cleavage, respectively.
- (E): Lamellar bodies are secretory organelles containing lipids that contribute to the epidermal water barrier. They fuse with the plasma membrane to release their contents. Defects in lamellar body formation or secretion are associated with conditions like ichthyosis, not EBS.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (D) - KRT5/Desmosome Linkage | Option (A) - Hemidesmosome/BM Linkage | Option (B) - KRT/Nuclear Lamina Linkage | Option (C) - Cadherin/ECM Interaction | Option (E) - Lamellar Body/PM Linkage |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :----------------------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- |
| **Defect Location**     | Intracellular plaque of desmosomes          | Hemidesmosome/Basement Membrane      | Nuclear Envelope/Cytoskeleton          | Cell-Cell Junction (Desmosome)      | Secretory Organelle/Plasma Membrane |
| **Primary Proteins**    | KRT5, Desmoplakin, Plakoglobin, Plakophilins | Integrin α6β4, Type VII Collagen     | Nesprins, SUN proteins, Lamins         | Desmogleins, Desmocollins           | Lipids, Ceramide Synthase           |
| **Cleavage Plane**      | Within basal keratinocytes (suprabasal)      | Junctional (below basal cells)       | N/A (not primary cause of EBS)         | Suprabasal (Pemphigus)               | N/A (not primary cause of EBS)         |
| **Clinical Condition**  | EBS Simplex                                 | JEB, DEB                             | Laminopathies                         | Pemphigus                            | Ichthyosis                          |
| **Genetic Basis**       | KRT5, KRT14 mutations                       | COL7A1, ITGA6, ITGB4 mutations       | LMNA mutations                        | Autoimmune (anti-Dsg antibodies)    | Various (e.g., TGM1)                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Epidermolysis Bullosa Simplex (EBS) results from defects in the structural integrity of the basal epidermis, specifically the linkage between keratin intermediate filaments (tonofilaments) and desmosomes, most commonly due to mutations in KRT5 or KRT14. This leads to intraepidermal cleavage within the basal layer upon minor trauma. Differentiate EBS from other forms of EB (JEB, DEB) based on the cleavage plane and affected structures (hemidesmosomes, basement membrane). Remember that EBS is typically non-inflammatory and does not involve autoimmune antibodies against desmosomal components.

### Question 108: Pemphigus Vulgaris: Autoantibodies against Desmoglein-3 and 1
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Pemphigus vulgaris is an autoimmune blistering disease characterized by autoantibodies against desmogleins (Dsg1 and Dsg3), leading to acantholysis (loss of cell-cell adhesion) within the epidermis. The specific location of lesions (mucous membranes vs. skin) correlates with the predominant desmoglein target (Dsg3 vs. Dsg1). This question tests the understanding of the anatomical distribution of Dsg3 expression and its clinical correlation in pemphigus vulgaris. (Pemphigus vulgaris 是一种自身免疫性大疱性疾病，其特征是对 desmoglein (Dsg1 和 Dsg3) 的自身抗体，导致表皮内细胞间连接的缺失 (角质分离)。病变部位 (黏膜与皮肤) 与主要 desmoglein 靶标 (Dsg3 与 Dsg1) 相关。本题考察对 Dsg3 表达的解剖分布及其在 pemphigus vulgaris 中的临床相关性的理解。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old female presents to the dermatology clinic with a 2-week history of painful oral lesions. Initially, she noticed small blisters on her palate and buccal mucosa, which rapidly ruptured, leaving extensive, shallow, erythematous erosions. These lesions make eating and speaking extremely difficult. Over the past week, similar, though less severe, lesions have appeared on her upper trunk, characterized by flaccid blisters that easily rupture upon minor trauma, leaving painful erosions. A skin biopsy from a perilesional area reveals intraepidermal acantholysis, specifically suprabasal splitting, with Tzanck cells (acantholytic keratinocytes) present. Direct immunofluorescence (DIF) shows intercellular IgG and C3 deposition throughout the epidermis. Indirect immunofluorescence (IIF) reveals circulating IgG autoantibodies against desmoglein 3 (Dsg3) and, to a lesser extent, desmoglein 1 (Dsg1). Given the initial and most prominent involvement of the oral mucosa, which anatomical layer is primarily affected by the autoantibodies in this patient's condition?

(A) Stratum basale
(B) Stratum spinosum
(C) Stratum granulosum
(D) Stratum corneum
(E) Dermal-epidermal junction

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 55-year-old female with painful oral lesions (palate, buccal mucosa) followed by skin lesions (upper trunk). Oral lesions precede skin lesions.
- **Lesion Characteristics:** Flaccid blisters that rupture easily, leaving painful erosions.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Intraepidermal acantholysis, suprabasal splitting, Tzanck cells. This indicates loss of adhesion between keratinocytes within the epidermis, specifically above the basal layer.
- **Immunofluorescence:** DIF shows intercellular IgG/C3 deposition throughout the epidermis. IIF shows antibodies against Dsg3 and Dsg1.
- **Key Clue:** The question asks which *anatomical layer* is primarily affected, given the prominent oral mucosal involvement and the autoantibody profile.
- **Deduction:** Pemphigus vulgaris involves autoantibodies against Dsg3 and Dsg1. Dsg3 is predominantly expressed in the lower layers of the epidermis, particularly the stratum basale and stratum spinosum, and is crucial for cell-cell adhesion in the mucous membranes. Dsg1 is more prevalent in the upper layers (stratum spinosum and granulosum) and is primarily involved in skin adhesion. The prominent oral mucosal involvement (which is rich in Dsg3) and the presence of antibodies against Dsg3 strongly suggest that the lower epidermal layers, where Dsg3 is highly expressed, are the primary site of acantholysis. Suprabasal splitting confirms this.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Epidermal Layers:** The epidermis consists of several layers (from deep to superficial): stratum basale (basal layer), stratum spinosum (prickle cell layer), stratum granulosum (granular layer), stratum lucidum (only in thick skin), and stratum corneum (horny layer).
- **Desmogleins:** Desmogleins (Dsg1, Dsg2, Dsg3) are cadherin proteins crucial for cell-cell adhesion in the epidermis. They form desmosomes, which connect adjacent keratinocytes.
- **Dsg3 Distribution:** Dsg3 is the major desmoglein in the lower epidermis (stratum basale and lower stratum spinosum) and is highly expressed in mucous membranes (oral mucosa, esophagus, etc.). It is essential for maintaining the integrity of these tissues.
- **Dsg1 Distribution:** Dsg1 is the major desmoglein in the upper epidermis (upper stratum spinosum, stratum granulosum) and is primarily involved in maintaining the integrity of the superficial layers of the skin.
- **Pemphigus Vulgaris Pathophysiology:** Autoantibodies against Dsg3 (and often Dsg1) disrupt desmosomal function, leading to loss of cell-cell adhesion (acantholysis).
- **Clinical Correlation:**
    - **Mucous Membrane Pemphigus (OMP):** Primarily involves mucous membranes (oral, esophageal, conjunctival). Caused mainly by anti-Dsg3 antibodies. Acantholysis occurs predominantly in the lower epidermis (stratum basale/spinosum), leading to suprabasal splitting.
    - **Pemphigus Vulgaris (PV):** Involves both mucous membranes and skin. Caused by antibodies against both Dsg3 and Dsg1. Acantholysis occurs throughout the epidermis, often suprabasal, but can extend higher.
    - **Pemphigus Foliaceus:** Primarily involves skin (superficial blisters). Caused mainly by anti-Dsg1 antibodies. Acantholysis occurs in the upper epidermis (stratum granulosum/corneum), leading to subcorneal or intragranular splitting.
- **In this case:** The patient has prominent oral involvement and antibodies against Dsg3, consistent with Mucous Membrane Pemphigus or Pemphigus Vulgaris with significant mucosal involvement. The biopsy shows suprabasal splitting, indicating acantholysis occurs above the stratum basale. The primary site of Dsg3 expression and the location of the splitting point to the stratum spinosum as the layer most affected by the loss of Dsg3-mediated adhesion.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Stratum basale: While Dsg3 is present in the stratum basale, the splitting occurs *above* this layer (suprabasal). The primary site of acantholysis is not the basal layer itself, but the connections between the basal cells and the cells above them (stratum spinosum).
- (C) Stratum granulosum: Dsg1 is more highly expressed in the stratum granulosum. While Dsg3 is also present, the primary target in mucous membranes and the location of the most severe acantholysis in this case (given prominent oral lesions and anti-Dsg3 antibodies) is lower down. Lesions primarily affecting the stratum granulosum would be more characteristic of pemphigus foliaceus (anti-Dsg1) or the upper part of pemphigus vulgaris.
- (D) Stratum corneum: The stratum corneum consists of dead, keratinized cells. Acantholysis occurs within the living layers of the epidermis, where desmosomes are functional. Pemphigus foliaceus can involve the stratum granulosum just below the corneum, but the primary site of acantholysis in this case is deeper.
- (E) Dermal-epidermal junction: This junction separates the epidermis from the dermis. Pemphigus vulgaris is an *intraepidermal* blistering disease, meaning the split occurs within the epidermis, not at the junction. Diseases affecting the dermal-epidermal junction include bullous pemphigoid (antibodies against hemidesmosomal proteins).

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Layer             | Key Features                                  | Dsg Expression | Pemphigus Involvement                                  |
|-------------------|-----------------------------------------------|----------------|-------------------------------------------------------|
| Stratum Basale    | Single layer of dividing keratinocytes         | Dsg3 (high)    | Primarily involved in Mucous Membrane Pemphigus (OMP) |
| Stratum Spinosum  | Multiple layers, desmosomes prominent          | Dsg3 (high), Dsg1 (increasing upwards) | Primary site of acantholysis in OMP & PV (suprabasal split) |
| Stratum Granulosum| Flattened cells, keratohyalin granules        | Dsg1 (high), Dsg3 (lower) | Primary site of acantholysis in Pemphigus Foliaceus (intragranular/subcorneal split) |
| Stratum Corneum   | Dead, keratinized cells                       | None           | Not directly involved in acantholysis                  |
| D-E Junction      | Basement membrane, hemidesmosomes             | None           | Involved in Bullous Pemphigoid                         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Pemphigus vulgaris involves autoantibodies against desmogleins, leading to intraepidermal acantholysis. The specific epidermal layer affected correlates with the desmoglein distribution and the clinical presentation. Prominent mucosal involvement and anti-Dsg3 antibodies target the lower epidermis (stratum basale and spinosum), causing suprabasal splitting. Remember the differential based on desmoglein target: Dsg3 primarily affects mucosa, Dsg1 primarily affects skin (superficial layers). Key differentials include bullous pemphigoid (D-E junction) and pemphigus foliaceus (upper epidermis/stratum granulosum).
